Well, the main thing is… I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me, even when I’m right there looking at them. Like at the museum, people will be telling me about the next art walk—where we’re meeting, what order things are happening in, that sort of thing—and I’ll be nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the middle of what they said.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to ignore anyone. I’m looking at them, I want to follow it, but then I have to ask, “Could you run that by me again?” And sometimes it feels like I’m doing that more than I should. That’s really what brought me in.

It’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.

So I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.

I’m looking at them, I want to follow it, but then I have to ask, “Could you run that by me again?” And sometimes it feels like I’m doing that more than I should. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble understanding what people say happen more when there's background noise or multiple people talking, or does it happen just as often in a quiet room with just one person?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like at the museum, people will be telling me about the next art walk—where we’re meeting, what order things are happening in, that sort of thing—and I’ll be nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to ignore anyone. I’m looking at them, I want to follow it, but then I have to ask, “Could you run that by me again?” And sometimes it feels like I’m doing that more than I should. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble understanding what people say happen more when there's background noise or multiple people talking, or does it happen just as often in a quiet room with just one person?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that you often need people to speak louder, or that you miss certain sounds like a phone ringing or the doorbell, even in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I need people to talk louder, exactly. It’s more like I hear that they’re talking, and I can tell there are words coming at me, but I don’t always hang onto the whole thing.\n\nAs far as sounds like a phone or doorbell, I can’t say I’ve noticed missing those in a quiet room. I mean, I suppose once in a while anybody might not catch something if they’re wrapped up in something else, but I haven’t thought, “Oh, I’m not hearing the phone,” or anything like that. It doesn’t feel like a volume problem to me, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have similar trouble following or remembering multi-step instructions when they're written down instead of spoken to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"When someone gives me a few things to handle for the volunteer group, I’ll do the first part, like printing the maps, but then I may forget to call the museum desk or leave the folders half-ready until someone reminds me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so, though it’s a little different when it’s written. If someone hands me something or texts me a list, I can look back at it, so in that way it’s easier than when they just say it out loud and it’s gone. But I still manage to miss pieces of it.

Like with the volunteer group, if somebody gives me a few things to take care of for one of the museum walks, I might do the first thing—say, print out the maps—and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then later it turns out I never called the museum desk like I was supposed to, or I started putting the folders together and left them kind of half-ready on the table until someone reminded me. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I usually mean to get through the whole thing. I just sort of latch onto one step and then the rest can slip away unless I keep checking back or someone gives me a nudge.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it’s not really new. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.

Even in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.

I guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.

I mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.

So I guess my answer is, not particularly. I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never, because I don’t remember every detail from when I was little, but it doesn’t stand out as a big part of it.

If someone hands me something or texts me a list, I can look back at it, so in that way it’s easier than when they just say it out loud and it’s gone. But I still manage to miss pieces of it.\n\nLike with the volunteer group, if somebody gives me a few things to take care of for one of the museum walks, I might do the first thing—say, print out the maps—and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then later it turns out I never called the museum desk like I was supposed to, or I started putting the folders together and left them kind of half-ready on the table until someone reminded me. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I usually mean to get through the whole thing. I just sort of latch onto one step and then the rest can slip away unless I keep checking back or someone gives me a nudge.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've had this kind of trouble following conversations and instructions since you were a child or teenager, or is this something that's really only started or gotten noticeably worse in recent months or years?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not really new. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nI guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble following instructions and conversations, were you also a particularly restless or fidgety kid—like having trouble sitting still, blurting out answers, or feeling like you were 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo I guess my answer is, not particularly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it does, though the museum is probably where I notice it most clearly because it’s something I cared about and had a role in. I used to feel pretty comfortable helping lead the art walks, getting pieces organized, knowing what needed to happen. But over time, I think people have noticed I don’t always follow through on the planning parts the way I should.

They haven’t made a big dramatic thing of it—no one sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this”—but I can tell some of the planning pieces have been quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take over the scheduling or the calling ahead, or they’ll double-check things I used to just be trusted to do. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.

The part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. It’s changed how much I trust myself with things I used to feel I could handle.

But I still manage to miss pieces of it.\n\nLike with the volunteer group, if somebody gives me a few things to take care of for one of the museum walks, I might do the first thing—say, print out the maps—and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then later it turns out I never called the museum desk like I was supposed to, or I started putting the folders together and left them kind of half-ready on the table until someone reminded me. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I usually mean to get through the whole thing. I just sort of latch onto one step and then the rest can slip away unless I keep checking back or someone gives me a nudge.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've had this kind of trouble following conversations and instructions since you were a child or teenager, or is this something that's really only started or gotten noticeably worse in recent months or years?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not really new. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nI guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble following instructions and conversations, were you also a particularly restless or fidgety kid—like having trouble sitting still, blurting out answers, or feeling like you were 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo I guess my answer is, not particularly. I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never, because I don’t remember every detail from when I was little, but it doesn’t stand out as a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with attention and following through on things affect you in other areas of your life too—like at home, in relationships, or at work—not just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, though the museum is probably where I notice it most clearly because it’s something I cared about and had a role in. I used to feel pretty comfortable helping lead the art walks, getting pieces organized, knowing what needed to happen. But over time, I think people have noticed I don’t always follow through on the planning parts the way I should.\n\nThey haven’t made a big dramatic thing of it—no one sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this”—but I can tell some of the planning pieces have been quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take over the scheduling or the calling ahead, or they’ll double-check things I used to just be trusted to do. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.\n\nThe part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. It’s changed how much I trust myself with things I used to feel I could handle.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0"], "note": "Childhood onset confirmed with clear inattentive symptoms (daydreaming, needing reminders, losing track of multi-step tasks) predating age 12, without hyperactive-impulsive features. Hearing loss ruled out (no volume issues, no missed environmental sounds). Symptoms are pervasive across settings (home, museum/work), consistent with predominantly inattentive ADHD rather than simple anxiety/unspecified symptoms (R41.8) or a general medical condition.", "is_final": false}

I’m not sure I can connect it that neatly, honestly. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.

It feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.

I guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, the main thing is… I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me, even when I’m right there looking at them. Like at the museum, people will be telling me about the next art walk—where we’re meeting, what order things are happening in, that sort of thing—and I’ll be nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to ignore anyone. I’m looking at them, I want to follow it, but then I have to ask, “Could you run that by me again?” And sometimes it feels like I’m doing that more than I should. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble understanding what people say happen more when there's background noise or multiple people talking, or does it happen just as often in a quiet room with just one person?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that you often need people to speak louder, or that you miss certain sounds like a phone ringing or the doorbell, even in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I need people to talk louder, exactly. It’s more like I hear that they’re talking, and I can tell there are words coming at me, but I don’t always hang onto the whole thing.\n\nAs far as sounds like a phone or doorbell, I can’t say I’ve noticed missing those in a quiet room. I mean, I suppose once in a while anybody might not catch something if they’re wrapped up in something else, but I haven’t thought, “Oh, I’m not hearing the phone,” or anything like that. It doesn’t feel like a volume problem to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have similar trouble following or remembering multi-step instructions when they're written down instead of spoken to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s a little different when it’s written. If someone hands me something or texts me a list, I can look back at it, so in that way it’s easier than when they just say it out loud and it’s gone. But I still manage to miss pieces of it.\n\nLike with the volunteer group, if somebody gives me a few things to take care of for one of the museum walks, I might do the first thing—say, print out the maps—and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then later it turns out I never called the museum desk like I was supposed to, or I started putting the folders together and left them kind of half-ready on the table until someone reminded me. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I usually mean to get through the whole thing. I just sort of latch onto one step and then the rest can slip away unless I keep checking back or someone gives me a nudge.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've had this kind of trouble following conversations and instructions since you were a child or teenager, or is this something that's really only started or gotten noticeably worse in recent months or years?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not really new. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nI guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble following instructions and conversations, were you also a particularly restless or fidgety kid—like having trouble sitting still, blurting out answers, or feeling like you were 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo I guess my answer is, not particularly. I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never, because I don’t remember every detail from when I was little, but it doesn’t stand out as a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with attention and following through on things affect you in other areas of your life too—like at home, in relationships, or at work—not just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, though the museum is probably where I notice it most clearly because it’s something I cared about and had a role in. I used to feel pretty comfortable helping lead the art walks, getting pieces organized, knowing what needed to happen. But over time, I think people have noticed I don’t always follow through on the planning parts the way I should.\n\nThey haven’t made a big dramatic thing of it—no one sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this”—but I can tell some of the planning pieces have been quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take over the scheduling or the calling ahead, or they’ll double-check things I used to just be trusted to do. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.\n\nThe part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. It’s changed how much I trust myself with things I used to feel I could handle.\n\nDoctor: Over these years, have you noticed periods of feeling persistently sad, anxious, or low in energy that seemed to coincide with when your concentration was at its worst?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that neatly, honestly. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.\n\nIt feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.\n\nI guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0"], "note": "Mood-related concentration issues ruled out as primary explanation; patient denies distinct mood episodes correlating with attention problems, reinforcing chronic inattentive-type presentation since childhood without hyperactive-impulsive features. Diagnosis remains ADHD, predominantly inattentive presentation.", "is_final": false}

But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow has your sleep been over the years—do you sleep well and wake up feeling rested, or do you have trouble with things like snoring, waking up a lot, or feeling tired during the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, I sleep, generally. I wouldn’t say I’ve had some obvious major sleep problem that I’m aware of, but I also don’t always wake up feeling wonderfully refreshed either. Some mornings I feel a bit slow getting going, but I’ve kind of thought of that as just… being me, or getting older, maybe.

As far as snoring or waking up a lot, I don’t have a very clear sense. Nobody has really pointed out to me, “You snore terribly,” or anything like that, at least not that I can recall. And I don’t usually remember waking up over and over all night. Sometimes I’ll wake up once and then go back to sleep, but I don’t know if that’s unusual.

During the day, I can get tired, but I’ve never been sure whether that’s sleep-related or just from the way the day goes. I guess I haven’t noticed a strong pattern where poor sleep explains the attention trouble. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I slept badly and that’s why I can’t focus.” It’s more that the focus issue has been there regardless. But no, I don’t have a clear sleep complaint to give you, if that’s what you’re asking.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, the main thing is… I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me, even when I’m right there looking at them. Like at the museum, people will be telling me about the next art walk—where we’re meeting, what order things are happening in, that sort of thing—and I’ll be nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’m not trying to ignore anyone. I’m looking at them, I want to follow it, but then I have to ask, “Could you run that by me again?” And sometimes it feels like I’m doing that more than I should. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble understanding what people say happen more when there's background noise or multiple people talking, or does it happen just as often in a quiet room with just one person?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that you often need people to speak louder, or that you miss certain sounds like a phone ringing or the doorbell, even in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I haven’t really noticed that I need people to talk louder, exactly. It’s more like I hear that they’re talking, and I can tell there are words coming at me, but I don’t always hang onto the whole thing.\n\nAs far as sounds like a phone or doorbell, I can’t say I’ve noticed missing those in a quiet room. I mean, I suppose once in a while anybody might not catch something if they’re wrapped up in something else, but I haven’t thought, “Oh, I’m not hearing the phone,” or anything like that. It doesn’t feel like a volume problem to me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have similar trouble following or remembering multi-step instructions when they're written down instead of spoken to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s a little different when it’s written. If someone hands me something or texts me a list, I can look back at it, so in that way it’s easier than when they just say it out loud and it’s gone. But I still manage to miss pieces of it.\n\nLike with the volunteer group, if somebody gives me a few things to take care of for one of the museum walks, I might do the first thing—say, print out the maps—and I’ll feel like, okay, I’m on it. But then later it turns out I never called the museum desk like I was supposed to, or I started putting the folders together and left them kind of half-ready on the table until someone reminded me. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I usually mean to get through the whole thing. I just sort of latch onto one step and then the rest can slip away unless I keep checking back or someone gives me a nudge.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've had this kind of trouble following conversations and instructions since you were a child or teenager, or is this something that's really only started or gotten noticeably worse in recent months or years?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not really new. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nI guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble following instructions and conversations, were you also a particularly restless or fidgety kid—like having trouble sitting still, blurting out answers, or feeling like you were 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo I guess my answer is, not particularly. I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never, because I don’t remember every detail from when I was little, but it doesn’t stand out as a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with attention and following through on things affect you in other areas of your life too—like at home, in relationships, or at work—not just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, though the museum is probably where I notice it most clearly because it’s something I cared about and had a role in. I used to feel pretty comfortable helping lead the art walks, getting pieces organized, knowing what needed to happen. But over time, I think people have noticed I don’t always follow through on the planning parts the way I should.\n\nThey haven’t made a big dramatic thing of it—no one sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this”—but I can tell some of the planning pieces have been quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take over the scheduling or the calling ahead, or they’ll double-check things I used to just be trusted to do. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.\n\nThe part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. It’s changed how much I trust myself with things I used to feel I could handle.\n\nDoctor: Over these years, have you noticed periods of feeling persistently sad, anxious, or low in energy that seemed to coincide with when your concentration was at its worst?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that neatly, honestly. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.\n\nIt feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.\n\nI guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over the years—do you sleep well and wake up feeling rested, or do you have trouble with things like snoring, waking up a lot, or feeling tired during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, I sleep, generally. I wouldn’t say I’ve had some obvious major sleep problem that I’m aware of, but I also don’t always wake up feeling wonderfully refreshed either. Some mornings I feel a bit slow getting going, but I’ve kind of thought of that as just… being me, or getting older, maybe.\n\nAs far as snoring or waking up a lot, I don’t have a very clear sense. Nobody has really pointed out to me, “You snore terribly,” or anything like that, at least not that I can recall. And I don’t usually remember waking up over and over all night. Sometimes I’ll wake up once and then go back to sleep, but I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nDuring the day, I can get tired, but I’ve never been sure whether that’s sleep-related or just from the way the day goes. I guess I haven’t noticed a strong pattern where poor sleep explains the attention trouble. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I slept badly and that’s why I can’t focus.” It’s more that the focus issue has been there regardless. But no, I don’t have a clear sleep complaint to give you, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.\n\nIt feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.\n\nI guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over the years—do you sleep well and wake up feeling rested, or do you have trouble with things like snoring, waking up a lot, or feeling tired during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, I sleep, generally. I wouldn’t say I’ve had some obvious major sleep problem that I’m aware of, but I also don’t always wake up feeling wonderfully refreshed either. Some mornings I feel a bit slow getting going, but I’ve kind of thought of that as just… being me, or getting older, maybe.\n\nAs far as snoring or waking up a lot, I don’t have a very clear sense. Nobody has really pointed out to me, “You snore terribly,” or anything like that, at least not that I can recall. And I don’t usually remember waking up over and over all night. Sometimes I’ll wake up once and then go back to sleep, but I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nDuring the day, I can get tired, but I’ve never been sure whether that’s sleep-related or just from the way the day goes. I guess I haven’t noticed a strong pattern where poor sleep explains the attention trouble. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I slept badly and that’s why I can’t focus.” It’s more that the focus issue has been there regardless. But no, I don’t have a clear sleep complaint to give you, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you currently use alcohol, cannabis, or any other recreational substances, and if so, how often?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

I’m not sure there’s much to tell there. I mean, I understand why you’re asking, but I don’t really have anything specific that comes to mind as a regular thing.

I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. I mean, I think I notice it more now because people expect me to keep track of things, and there’s more going on, but when I think back, it was there when I was a kid.\n\nEven in grade school, my report cards would say things like I “daydreamed” or “needed reminders to finish work.” I remember that because my mother would get frustrated, not in a mean way exactly, but she’d have to sit beside me when I was doing homework to keep me moving along. And that was before I was twelve, so it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nI guess at the time everybody just thought I was kind of drifting off or not applying myself, but looking back, it feels like the same sort of thing—someone would tell me what to do, or I’d be working on something, and unless somebody kept me on track, I’d lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Besides having trouble following instructions and conversations, were you also a particularly restless or fidgety kid—like having trouble sitting still, blurting out answers, or feeling like you were 'driven by a motor'?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I was like that, no. At least, that’s not the way I remember myself. I don’t have a strong memory of being the kid who was constantly jumping up or running around the classroom or blurting things out. If anything, when people talked about me, it was more that I was off in my own head, you know, kind of staring out the window or not getting started.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I fidgeted some—kids do—but I don’t remember teachers saying I couldn’t sit still, or that I was always interrupting. And my parents didn’t describe me as being “driven by a motor” or anything like that. That phrase sounds more like a very active kid, and I don’t think that was me. I was more… slow to get going, maybe, or mentally somewhere else.\n\nSo I guess my answer is, not particularly. I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never, because I don’t remember every detail from when I was little, but it doesn’t stand out as a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with attention and following through on things affect you in other areas of your life too—like at home, in relationships, or at work—not just at the museum?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, though the museum is probably where I notice it most clearly because it’s something I cared about and had a role in. I used to feel pretty comfortable helping lead the art walks, getting pieces organized, knowing what needed to happen. But over time, I think people have noticed I don’t always follow through on the planning parts the way I should.\n\nThey haven’t made a big dramatic thing of it—no one sat me down and said, “You can’t handle this”—but I can tell some of the planning pieces have been quietly shifted away from me. Someone else will take over the scheduling or the calling ahead, or they’ll double-check things I used to just be trusted to do. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.\n\nThe part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. It’s changed how much I trust myself with things I used to feel I could handle.\n\nDoctor: Over these years, have you noticed periods of feeling persistently sad, anxious, or low in energy that seemed to coincide with when your concentration was at its worst?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can connect it that neatly, honestly. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.\n\nIt feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.\n\nI guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over the years—do you sleep well and wake up feeling rested, or do you have trouble with things like snoring, waking up a lot, or feeling tired during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that in a clean way. I mean, I sleep, generally. I wouldn’t say I’ve had some obvious major sleep problem that I’m aware of, but I also don’t always wake up feeling wonderfully refreshed either. Some mornings I feel a bit slow getting going, but I’ve kind of thought of that as just… being me, or getting older, maybe.\n\nAs far as snoring or waking up a lot, I don’t have a very clear sense. Nobody has really pointed out to me, “You snore terribly,” or anything like that, at least not that I can recall. And I don’t usually remember waking up over and over all night. Sometimes I’ll wake up once and then go back to sleep, but I don’t know if that’s unusual.\n\nDuring the day, I can get tired, but I’ve never been sure whether that’s sleep-related or just from the way the day goes. I guess I haven’t noticed a strong pattern where poor sleep explains the attention trouble. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I slept badly and that’s why I can’t focus.” It’s more that the focus issue has been there regardless. But no, I don’t have a clear sleep complaint to give you, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently use alcohol, cannabis, or any other recreational substances, and if so, how often?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure there’s much to tell there. I mean, I understand why you’re asking, but I don’t really have anything specific that comes to mind as a regular thing.\n\nAlcohol… I suppose there may be the occasional drink in a social setting or with a meal, but I wouldn’t describe myself as someone who uses it in any major or routine way. Cannabis or other recreational drugs, no, not in any way that’s part of my life.\n\nSo I don’t think substances are a big factor here, at least not that I’ve noticed."
  }

Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s definitely most noticeable at the museum when there’s people around and things going on, but the part that bothers me is that it’s not only when it’s noisy. I mean, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk—like where we’re supposed to meet, what the plan is, what order we’re doing things—and I’m looking at them, I’m trying to listen, and then I realize I missed the middle of it.\n\nSo I guess background noise probably makes it worse, sure, but even one person talking to me, if they’re giving me a few details, I can lose part of what they said and then I have to ask them to run it by me again. That’s the embarrassing part, because I don’t want them to think I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. And honestly, I can’t really blame them, because sometimes I have dropped the ball.\n\nThe part that bothers me is I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the walks the way I used to. I still like doing them, and I still care about the group, but there’s this feeling now of, “Am I going to miss something? Did I forget a step? Are people watching to see if I mess up?” So yes, it affects me beyond just one little mistake here or there. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I can connect it that neatly, honestly. I mean, I’ve had times where I felt discouraged or worried about things, especially when I realized I’d forgotten something or someone else had to step in, but I don’t know that there were clear periods where my concentration got worse because I was sad or anxious.\n\nIt feels more like the concentration problem has just kind of been there in the background for a long time. Some days are better than others, of course, and if I’m stressed I probably do worse, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a definite stretch where I was persistently low or anxious and then that explained it.\n\nI guess what I know is that the attention trouble seems to come first, or at least it’s the thing I notice most. Then I feel bad about the consequences afterward. But I’m not sure I’d describe it as mood causing the concentration problems.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about sleep quality and possible sleep-related causes of attention problems, which is not covered by the listed ADHD symptom profile items.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
